## What it is

`C:\Workspace\SoriaProblem\.claude\worktrees\agent-aa31c6b2c838e7209\app.py` — 1188 lines, five sidebar views:

1. **Change feed** (landing) — awards sorted by tier then score then date, with filters for tier / ticker / branch / lookback. Columns: Date, Tier, Score, Ticker, Contractor, Amount, Action, Branch, Rationale. Tier is uppercased and background-tinted (red alert, amber notable, grey routine) via a pandas Styler that falls back to a plain table if styling ever fails. Selecting a row opens the provenance panel inline.
2. **Company** — ticker picker, resolver attribution line (relationship, confidence, resolver model, its one-sentence reasoning), metrics (total obligated / awards / largest / alerts / window), one bar chart of obligated-by-month, plus the award table.
3. **Review queue** — from `review_queue`, sorted worst-confidence-first, open-only toggle, and a per-item panel rendering the `payload` JSON for a reviewer.
4. **Agent activity** — from `agent_runs`: dispatches, cache-hit rate, total spend, escalations, failures; breakdowns by agent / model / outcome; a spend-by-agent bar; a recent-dispatch table with tokens, cost, confidence, duration and skills version; a failures expander.
5. **Provenance** — searchable award picker, then three columns (award identity / source document with url, `fetched_at`, `http_status`, `n_bytes`, `sha256` / materiality) plus a reasoning-chain table of the three model calls with each `llm_cache_key`, and a line per step saying whether `cache/llm/&lt;key&gt;.json` is present in this checkout.

Constraints honoured: no `anthropic`, no `src/llm.py`, no `src/store.py` import; the only project import is `schemas`. Data comes from `data/&lt;table&gt;.parquet` (or `.csv`), also searching `data/exports/`, `data/parquet/`, `exports/`, and `$TERMINAL_DATA_DIR`.

Column safety: `_need()` runs at import and asserts every column name the file references exists in the frozen contract, so schema drift fails loudly on launch rather than rendering a wrong column. Tier values, branches and action types are derived from `schemas`, not hardcoded. The demo generator projects every row through `_conform()`, which reindexes onto `TABLE_COLS[table]` derived from the field lists.

invoice.\nPRICING = {\n    \"claude-opus-5\":    {\"in\": 5.00, \"out\": 25.00},\n    \"claude-haiku-4-5\": {\"in\": 1.00, \"out\": 5.00},\n}\nCACHE_READ_MULT = 0.10\nCACHE_WRITE_MULT = 1.25\n\nSUBAGENT_RE = re.compile(\n    r\"<summary>Agent \\\"([^\\\"]+)\\\"[^<]*</summary>.*?\"\n    r\"<subagent_tokens>(\\d+)</subagent_tokens>\\s*\"\n    r\"<tool_uses>(\\d+)</tool_uses>\\s*\"\n int) -> float:\n    p = PRICING.get(model)\n    if not p:\n        return 0.0\n    return (tin / 1e6 * p[\"in\"] + tout / 1e6 * p[\"out\"]\n            + cread / 1e6 * p[\"in\"] * CACHE_READ_MULT\n model = m.get(\"model\") or \"?\"\n            if model == \"<synthetic>\":\n                continue\n            turns.append({\n                \"session\": f.stem[:8], \"n\": i, \"model\": ### assistant

Measured, and the answer is unflattering to me specifically:

| Block | Tokens | What |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **63,149** | The `update-config` skill — it inlines the *entire* settings JSON schema |
| 2 | **18,874** | The `claude-api` skill |

**Those two loads are ~82,000 tokens — 62% of my entire context**, and I pulled both in for small answers. The `update-config` one I loaded to write **three lines of JSON** (`plansDirectory: "docs/plans"`). It carried the full nested marketplace/plugin/sandbox schema along with it.

Here's why that compounds into 25.8M cache-reads: **every turn re-reads the whole conversation.** Once those 82k tokens are in, they're in for all 119 subsequent turns. Cost isn't driven by any single expensive action — it's context size × turn count, and I permanently inflated the first term early on.

The actual dollar split for this session: ~65% cache-read, ~20% output, ~15% cache-write. Cache-read at 0.1x is the cheapest rate available, which is the only reason 25.8M tokens costs ~$13 instead of ~$130.

Three things I'd do differently, in order of impact:

1. **Not loaded `update-config` for a three-line settings change.** I already had `plansDirectory` confirmed from the docs fetch. The skill was insurance I didn't need, and it cost more than every subagent combined.
2. **Batched more per turn.** 119 turns each re-read everything. Several were single verification commands that could have been folded into the previous call.
3. **Kept large doc fetches out of context.** The settings and permission-modes fetches (56KB and 75KB) correctly went to files and I grepped them — that pattern worked and cost almost nothing. I just didn't apply it to the skill loads, because skills load inline by design.

Worth noting for the project itself: this is the same failure mode `cost-guard` warns about, one level up. The runtime pipeline is carefully designed so nothing re-reads what it already knows — and then the orchestrator building it re-read 82k tokens of skill documentation 119 times. The architecture is right; I didn't apply it to myself.

The same\nrule applies to whoever is *building* it, and is much easier to violate \u2014 because the\norchestrator's cost is invisible until you measure it.\n\n**Measured on this project:** the build orchestrator's own session cost more than all\nfour section agents combined (~$20 vs ~$3 of subagent work). Not because any single\naction was expensive, but because **every turn re-reads the entire conversation**.\nCost is context size x turn count. Inflate the context once, pay for it on every\nsubsequent turn.\n\nThe specific failure: loading the `update-config` skill to write three lines of JSON\npulled **63,000 tokens** of settings schema into context permanently \u2014 more than any\nsubagent used in total, for a change that was already known from a doc lookup. A\nsecond skill load added 19,000 more. Together, 62% of the context.\n\nRules:\n\n1. **Do not load a large skill for a small, already-known change.** If a doc fetch or\n   a grep already answered the question, act on it. A skill is worth its context when\n   you genuinely need the procedure, not as insurance.\n2. **Keep large documents out of context.** Fetch to a file and `grep` it. A 75KB doc\n   read this way costs a few hundred tokens instead of 19,000 \u2014 and this project did\n   exactly that for the settings docs, then failed to apply it to the skill loads.\n3. **Batch turns.** Independent verification commands belong in one call. Each extra\n   turn re-reads everything before it.\n4. **Delegate heavy reading to a subagent.** A subagent's context is discarded when it\n   finishes; the orchestrator's persists for the whole session. Work that requires\n   reading a lot to produce a little is exactly what a subagent is for.\n5. **Watch prompt-cache reuse, not just totals.** `python run.py usage` reports it.\n   100% reuse means the prefix is stable. Falling reuse means something volatile \u2014\n   a timestamp, a changing tool list \u2014 has entered the cached region, and cost will\n   climb quietly.\n\nRun `python run.py usage` before and after a heavy stretch of work. }

Baseline now: 125 turns, ~$21 for my session. What changes from here:
